He instead proposes reverse engineering parameter count by using the fact that neural networks can only store a linear number of facts in parameter count.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8ne61o2xybo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8ne61o2xybo">[7]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Unfortunately, this isn’t as simple as counting </span><i><span>all</span></i><span>&nbsp;the facts:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflhruy9lnj5f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlhruy9lnj5f">[8]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;for one, doing that exhaustively is intractable.</span></p><p><span>Li builds a set of questions ("Incompressible Knowledge Probes," IKP) testing factual knowledge with varying degrees of obscurity. Probes come from four sources: GPT-5-generated questions, Wikidata SPARQL pulls, DBLP/OpenAlex researcher records, and a small set of hand-curated questions. Li calls these "probes," but to avoid confusion we'll just call them questions.</span></p><p><span>Li claims six contributions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>He introduces the IKP dataset, in order to measure incompressible facts. </span></b><span>This is distinguished from procedural knowledge (e.g. how to write code), which is likely compressible.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>He regresses model parameters against IKP performance, and finds a strong linear relationship between adjusted IKP performance and model parameter count on 89 open source models. </span></b><span>He also confirms that IKP outperforms MMLU, GPQA, and SimpleQA at predicting parameter count. We think this result generally holds up, though we believe the exact strength of the claimed relationship to be overstated.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>He “falsifies” the “densing law” results from previous work.</span></b><span>&nbsp;We agree that the densing law</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4hkgij7j5ae"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4hkgij7j5ae">[9]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp; paper is indeed very suspect (if there’s interest, we can detail why in a follow-up post). However, the densing law paper is not directly “falsified"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5l2g15lwoq9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5l2g15lwoq9">[10]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;by Li’s results; a more correct reading of which is that, controlling for parameter count, open-source LLMs are not getting better on his IKP dataset.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>He uses the IKP &lt;-&gt; model parameter regression to estimate the parameters of closed-source frontier models and the “effective” parameters of MoEs. </span></b><span>These results are headlined by GPT-5.5 (~9.7T) and Claude Opus 4.6 (~5.3T). He also shows that for Mixture-of-Experts models, total parameters predict factual knowledge much better than active parameters (R² of 0.79 vs 0.51).</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>He uses similarity on responses on the IKP dataset to identify models</span></b><span>&nbsp;</span><b><span>with the same base model vs full retrains. </span></b><span>Specifically, he combines rare-fact Jaccard overlap with "hallucination similarity" (the rate at which two models produce the same wrong answer on rare facts) into a Hallucination Similarity Score, which he claims separates weight-sharing siblings, post-training lineages, and full retrains across closed vendors without requiring model weights. We did not investigate these results in detail, so we can't speak to whether the lineage clusters in the paper's Figure 5 are correct.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhc9rd7w84c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhc9rd7w84c">[11]</a></sup></span></li><li value="6"><b><span>He open-sourced his code on GitHub. </span></b><span>We appreciate this a lot because it 1) greatly simplified the process of reproducing his results and 2) made it much easier to identify possible methodological issues with his work.</span></li></ol><p><br /></p><h2><span>The IKP dataset</span></h2><p><span>The IKP dataset consists of 1400 questions, divided evenly into 7 tiers of obscurity/difficulty (200 questions per tier). There are four sources of questions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>GPT-5-generated candidates (401 questions): These are questions generated by asking GPT-5 to generate factual questions with a few provided examples. These compose the bulk of T1-T2 questions, though some make it to T3-T4. Example: </span></li></ul><blockquote><p><span>[T2] "Who composed the ballet 'Giselle'?"</span><br /><span>Gold Answer: Adolphe Adam</span></p></blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span>Wikidata SPARQL questions (557 questions): These questions are drawn from the wikidata database, and involve asking about the founding years for institutions, capital cities of countries, location of headquarters, and geographic facts. These mainly populate T3-T7 (only 11 of the wikidata questions are in T1 or T2). Example:</span></li></ul><blockquote><p><span>&nbsp;[T4] "In what year was National Pingtung University of Education founded?"</span></p><p><span>Gold: 1940</span></p></blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span>DBLP / OpenAlex researcher questions (345 questions): These questions ask the model to "name the subfield and one paper, system, institution, or co-author for [researcher]". Most of these are in T5-T7. Example:</span></li></ul><blockquote><p><span>[T5] "In computer science, what is the research subfield of Martina Zitterbart, and name one paper, system, institution, or co-author associated with their work? If you don't know who this person is, say so."</span></p><p><span>Gold: computer networking</span></p><p><span>[papers, coauthors, and affiliations from OpenAlex omitted for brevity]</span></p></blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span>97 manual or supplementary additions from the author’s previous work to balance T1–T4 coverage. Examples:</span></li></ul><blockquote><p><span>[T1] "What is the capital of Portugal?" → Lisbon</span></p><p><span>[T2] "What is the largest lake in Africa?" → Victoria</span></p><p><span>[T3] "Who composed the opera 'The Magic Flute'?" → Mozart</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The difficulty of each tier is empirically calibrated against 6 "landmark" models. These models consist of 5 open-weight models ranging from Qwen 2.5 0.5B (T1) up to Kimi K2.5 1T (T5), as well as Gemini 3.1 Pro (T6). A question is assigned to tier k if the k-th landmark answers it correctly but the (k−1)-th landmark does not.&nbsp;T7 is reserved for questions no landmark model gets right.  </span></p><p><span>As we’ll later note, both the wikidata and researcher question datasets (which comprise over 900 of the 1400 questions, including </span><i><span>all</span></i><span>&nbsp;questions from T5-T7) have fairly significant quality issues.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu340z5v477b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu340z5v477b">[12]</a></sup></span><span> For example, both wikidata and the researcher question sets contain many ambiguous questions resulting from name-space collisions (e.g. multiple researchers or locations that have the same name). Other wikidata questions are either underspecified or have ambiguous answers: e.g. there's a question asking about Oxford's founding, but </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Oxford"><span>Oxford received its royal charter in 1248, but there’s been evidence of teaching at Oxford in 1096 and the university arguably could’ve existed earlier</span></a><span>. Some of the questions also reference outdated information, or only consider </span><i><span>one</span></i><span> author correct for multi-authored works. This complicates the interpretation of the results.</span></p><h2><span>IKP scoring and regression methodology</span></h2><p><span>For each of a model’s answers, Li scores its responses on either a 3 or 4-point scale:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>STRONG / CORRECT = +1.0</span></li><li value="2"><span>WEAK = +0.5 (Reserved for researcher questions where the model provides the right subfield but no supporting evidence.)</span></li><li value="3"><span>REFUSAL = 0</span></li><li value="4"><span>WRONG = λ, where λ = −1.0 by default (the "hallucination penalty")</span></li></ul><p><span>The hallucination penalty is added in order to discourage guessing (though it also penalize models who know the answers to questions that have incorrect gold answers). Each of the 7 tiers’ score is the mean over its 200 questions, and a model's overall "penalized accuracy" is the unweighted mean of the seven tier scores. To calculate penalized accuracy, the per-tier scores are floored at 0 in the released data, even though the paper text explicitly claims they are not floored "to preserve the bluff signal in the calibration." This is one of the methodology inconsistencies we'll come back to, as the choice meaningfully changes the slope of the fit.</span></p><p><span>The judge is Gemini 3 Flash Preview at temperature 0, and all target models are run once at temperature 0. Note that this is fairly non-standard for model evaluations (and many reasoning model providers explicitly discourage running their models with t=0).</span></p><p><span>The headline regression is a one-line OLS:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>This OLS is fit on 89 open-weight models with known parameter counts, ranging from SmolLM2-135M up to DeepSeek V4 Pro at 1.6T. Li reports α = 0.147, β = +0.132, R² = 0.917, with leave-one-out median fold error of 1.59× and a 90% prediction interval factor of 3.0×. Inverting the regression gives a parameter-count estimate for any target model: N̂ = 10^((A − β) / α).</span></p><p><span>For Mixture-of-Experts models, total parameters predict factual knowledge meaningfully better than active parameters (R² = 0.79 vs 0.51).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx6cmmgcbgc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx6cmmgcbgc">[13]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;</span></p><h2><span>IKP performance doesn't improve over time</span></h2><p><span>The</span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s42256-025-01137-0"><span>&nbsp;densing law paper (Xiao et al. 2024)</span></a><span>&nbsp;introduces "capability density", defined as the ratio of a model's effective parameter size to its actual parameter size. Here, "effective size" is the parameter count a reference model would need to match the target's downstream score. Across 29 open-source base models, they fit </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and report </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> per day, which they translate to "the maximum capability density of LLMs doubles approximately every 3.3 months.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxo842f3qlsp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxo842f3qlsp">[14]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;</span></p><p><span>To test whether or not a similar law applies to the IKP questions, Li adds release date as a covariate to the IKP regression:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>If Xiao et al.'s densing law applied to the IKP questions, then γ should be about +0.0117/month (the value that produces the claimed 3.3-month density doubling). Across 96 dated open-weight models, Li fits γ = −0.0010/month, 95% CI [−0.0031, +0.0008] — statistically indistinguishable from zero, and rejects the naive application of densing law at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span></p><p><span>This result stands up to all of the stress testing we performed. We refit the regression with vendor fixed effects (22 vendor dummies), family fixed effects (33 family dummies), without thinking-mode variants, dropping the open-weight tier-landmark models (anti-circularity check), and under both flooring regimes for the per-tier scores. In every specification γ stays within ±0.004/month of zero, and the +0.0117/month densing prediction is rejected with effective certainty. So whatever else the paper does, this result holds.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp8jll77oebl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp8jll77oebl">[15]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>That being said, we believe the right way to read this result is: holding parameter count fixed, factual recall on rare entities has not improved across open-weight model generations from 2023 through April 2026. Controlling for parameter count, procedural benchmarks like MMLU and HumanEval have improved over the same window, often dramatically.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdwwq9ri67p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndwwq9ri67p">[16]</a></sup></span><span> Li falsifies Xiao et al.'s densing law on the IKP dataset, but not in general, given that the densing law was not intended to cover factual recall capacity. </span></p><h1><span>Methodological Issues with the IKP paper</span></h1><p><span>The paper and codebase have a number of methodological issues, both across dataset construction, judging methodology, and reporting of results. The two main methodological issues that impact the results are the use of per-tier flooring for scores (contrary to the paper’s claims) and questions with ambiguous and incorrect answers. When we adjust for these issues in our replication, the headline numbers change significantly.</span></p><h2><span>Per-tier floors to the scoring</span></h2><p><span>When scoring the models, each probe score is measured:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>STRONG / CORRECT = +1.0</span></li><li value="2"><span>WEAK = +0.5</span></li><li value="3"><span>REFUSAL = 0</span></li><li value="4"><span>WRONG = λ, where λ = −1.0</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7fbou61warv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7fbou61warv">[17]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;(the "hallucination penalty")</span></li></ul><p><span>In the paper section 4.3 says </span><i><span>"Per-tier scores are not floored at zero in the released results … to preserve the bluff signal in the calibration."</span></i><span>&nbsp;Flooring means that if it would normally go negative due to wrong answers, it would instead be held at 0. While they claim that the results are not floored, they in fact are floored, both for the reported values in the paper and in the repository. </span></p><p><span>Applying per-tier flooring makes the scores of smaller models much higher: relative to larger models, they hallucinate more and refuse less when they don't know the answer, but these hallucinations get rounded up to zero. Removing the flooring makes smaller models have lower accuracy at a given parameter count, and thus decreases the slope of log parameters against accuracy. Specifically:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Using accuracies floored at zero, as in the author's results:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>For very small models (&lt;1B), accuracy is consistently floored up to around 0.05.</span></li><li value="2"><span>For large models (&gt;500B), accuracy is around 0.57.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The log parameter/accuracy slope is 6.79.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref33fnw78g87s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn33fnw78g87s">[18]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Using unfloored accuracies, as described in the paper:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>For very small models (&lt;1B), accuracy drops to around −0.39.</span></li><li value="2"><span>For large models (&gt;500B), the accuracy drops a small amount to 0.51.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The log parameter/accuracy slope drops to 3.56, around half of the floored slope.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>Addressing this inconsistency also causes R² to drop from 0.917 → 0.815 and the 90% prediction interval span (95 percentile param count/5 percentile param count) to widen from 3.0 to 5.7. Notably, the </span><i><span>slope </span></i><span>of log parameter count relative to performance drops substantially, from 6.79 to 3.56. This means the large parameter counts for frontier models in Li’s original paper are largely an artifact of the flooring (or more cynically, an undocumented “code-level optimization”). </span></p><p><span> </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="calibration_scatter.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777661835/lexical_client_uploads/t3iu4vjthevwsb5ao6yp.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>Addressing the two major issues we identified leads to a substantially different slope compared to the original fit in the paper, and leads to a slightly lower R².  See the appendix for further details.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h2><span>Ambiguous/incorrect answers to hard questions</span></h2><p><span>For the </span><b><span>researcher questions,</span></b><span>&nbsp;Li filters out two-character Chinese names and single-initial given names (Section 4.1). Unfortunately, manual inspection of some randomly sampled questions revealed two issues this filter doesn't catch:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>First, researchers whose name is shared by multiple distinct CS researchers with non-trivial citation counts. We re-queried OpenAlex for every researcher in the 345-probe set and counted distinct profiles sharing the name with ≥50 citations each. Examples where reasonable disagreement is genuinely possible: Stjepan Picek (17 OpenAlex profiles, 2 high-cite), Zhendong Su (24 profiles, 4 high-cite), Zhiguo Ding (25 profiles, 6 high-cite). Across tiers, we flagged 86 of 345 (24.9%) researcher questions  as ambiguous: T3: 11/35 (31%), T4: 11/51 (22%), T5: 25/100 (25%), T6: 14/59 (24%), T7: 25/100 (25%).</span></li><li value="2"><span>Second, researchers whose primary subfield is contested or has shifted over time. Dan Suciu's field is given in the gold answers as "programming languages," but his most-cited and most-recent work is in databases. Under their scoring system, a model that says "databases" is marked WRONG despite being arguably more correct. </span></li></ul><p><span>For</span><b><span>&nbsp;wikidata questions</span></b><span>, Li applied a “10-round audit/repair cycle” (section 7.7). Unfortunately, it seems that this repair cycle failed to catch at least two types of issue:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>First, </span></b><span>we noted that for a large number of entities, there still remained ambiguities regarding which entity the question referred to. For example, one wikidata question asks "In what country is the cape Cape Simpson located?" with answer "Greenland", even though there also exist "Cape Simpson"s </span><a href="https://alaska.guide/map/cape-simpson" rel="noreferrer"><span>in Alaska</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Simpson" rel="noreferrer"><span>in the Antarctic</span></a><span>. Similarly, there are multiple rivers named Běla in Czechia other than the one assumed in the gold answer (</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C4%9Bl%C3%A1_(Divok%C3%A1_Orlice)" rel="noreferrer"><span>example 1</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biela_(river)" rel="noreferrer"><span>example 2</span></a><span>). We found that of the 557 wiki questions, 45 referenced an entity that shared a label with 2 or more other entities. </span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Second, </span></b><span>there remain other genuine semantic ambiguities. For example, the UT Austin School of Nursing question (gold: founded 1890) is technically right but ambiguous. The 1890 school was founded in Galveston, not Austin. UT Austin offered its first nursing courses in 1960, and the program was officially incorporated into UT Austin in 1976; models often give the two other answers. Other ambiguities we found were questions where multi-author works only had a single author as the gold answer. For example, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_Barrier_(film)" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Greater Barrier Film</span></a><span> has 4 listed screenwriters on Wikipedia (and 3 on some other sources), of which only </span><i><span>one</span></i><span> is considered the right answer. These are different failure modes from name collisions, and a more thorough audit would no doubt find more of them. </span></li></ul><p><span>For </span><b><span>manually generated</span></b><span>&nbsp;questions, we inspected those where the models consistently did much worse than the tier would suggest, and found two incorrect questions: one on the highest peak in Bangladesh (whose answer changed from the IKP gold answer of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keokradong" rel="noreferrer"><span>Keokradong</span></a><span> to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka_Haphong" rel="noreferrer"><span>Saka Haphong</span></a><span> with more survey data) and another on the founding year of Mongolian People’s Revolutionary Party (different sources cite </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongolian_People%27s_Party" rel="noreferrer"><span>1920</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://www.socialistinternational.org/our-meetings/committees/asia-pacific-1999-2011/strengthening-democracy-and-pursuing-sustainable-and-fair-economic-growth-in-the-asia-pacific-region/statement-on-mongolia/" rel="noreferrer"><span>1921</span></a><span>). We excluded these two from our analysis. </span></p><p><span>Interestingly, these possible issues are noted by Li in Appendix H. However, he does not attempt to quantify how many questions are ambiguous or incorrect, nor how large the impact is if you were to remove the ambiguous questions. We do that here.</span></p><p><span>We attempted to remove the ambiguous or incorrect questions we noticed, though we note that we were unable to remove all Wikidata questions involving semantic ambiguities besides name collisions as we didn't have the time to check the answer to </span><i><span>every</span></i><span> single question manually. </span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Source</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Number of Questions</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Flagged ambiguous</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Heuristic</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>LLM-generated</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>401</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Visual spot check was performed across all four tiers with an LLM judge. The questions seem well formed.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Researcher</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>345</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>86 (24.9%)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>OpenAlex shows ≥2 distinct researchers with ≥50 citations sharing the name.</span></p></td></tr><tr style="height: 180px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Wikidata</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>557</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>45 (8.08%)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>≥3 Wikidata entities share the same label. (We found other categories of ambiguities that we could not programmatically remove.)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Manually generated</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>97</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>2 (2.05%)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>We manually inspected the manually generated questions where the models performed much worse than expected, and confirmed that 2 of the questions were incorrect.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Total questions audited</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>1,400</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>131 (9.4%)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p><p><br /></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p><h2><span>Corrected model parameter estimates</span></h2><p><span>We attempt to fix the two methodological issues we identified above, by removing questions that have ambiguous answers from the various datasets, and also by removing the flooring from the accuracy estimates. We then recalculate scores across all the models measured in the paper. We present the newly calculated scores for 8 large frontier or near-frontier models; a larger list of predicted model parameter counts is included in the appendix.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="forest_plot.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777664196/lexical_client_uploads/jkbtxndhemagi6oyr1kx.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>Parameter estimates comparing our methodology (blue) with the original paper's (red) with 90% prediction intervals (log scale). The green stars are true parameter sizes where known.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Overall, the estimates drop massively for the most capable frontier models (observing a nearly 10x difference in Gemini 3.1-pro) and for some of the smaller models we see a modest increase in score.</span></p><h1><span>Possible methodological issues that mattered less than we thought</span></h1><h2><span>Thinking vs non-thinking</span></h2><p><span>In the original work, models would often behave much better when thinking was enabled rather than not. This led to parameter estimates that were off by as much as 4.9x: Grok-4.20 was estimated to have 110B parameters without thinking, and 540B parameters with. Claude Opus 4.6 was estimated to have 2.4T parameters without thinking, but 5.3T with thinking enabled, a 2.2x difference.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/82fc9544fb3039cf6e88f3a4f6ffd4af1e59641470f0cd396e568def3090807f/omoqldqedmstbvl6nb9p" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>This figure shows the difference in scores between the estimated parameter counts when the model was and was not given additional tokens in which to think, under Li's original methodology.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The headline results in the paper obscure this difference, as they generally report the </span><i><span>maximum</span></i><span>&nbsp;parameter count of either the thinking and non-thinking versions of the same model.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="thinking_old_vs_new.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777662348/lexical_client_uploads/pxezy0s9lcuwmbnsi1wc.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>This figure shows the difference in scores between the estimated parameter counts when the model was and was not given additional tokens in which to think, using our updated methodology. We find that with the modifications the scores are significantly closer together than the floored version presented in the original paper.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Interestingly, after removing the arbitrary flooring, we generally observe much smaller score differences between frontier models with and without thinking enabled. Grok-4.20’s thinking multiplier dropped to 3.9x, while Claude Opus 4.6’s dropped to 1.2x. We unfortunately did not have the time to investigate </span><i><span>why</span></i><span>&nbsp;the thinking gap decreased after flooring was removed.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqqbw1apdzi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqqbw1apdzi">[19]</a></sup></span><span> That being said, we believe that this is some evidence that, on the IKP benchmark, enabling or disabling thinking does not have that much of an effect on the estimated parameter counts of frontier models. </span></p><h2><span>Different accuracy metrics used in some repository JSON files</span></h2><p><span>We observed that the penalized accuracy metric used to score the models in some of the JSON files in the repo was different from what was shown in the paper. We subsequently investigated whether these different accuracy metrics affected the results, but found that the aberrant JSON scores were not used to produce any of the figures or tables in the paper. That is, the different accuracy metrics did not affect any of Li’s results as presented in the paper.</span></p><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>In this work, we examined the robustness of both the methodology and results of Li’s “Incompressible Knowledge Probes” paper. We identified two main methodological issues with the work: the per-tier flooring that exists in the code </span><i><span>despite </span></i><span>the paper claiming otherwise and the large fraction of ambiguous questions, especially in higher tiers of difficulty. We also note two questionable methodological choices that </span><i><span>do not</span></i><span>&nbsp;impact the results significantly: the performance gap between thinking and non-thinking models was much smaller than we initially thought, and the different accuracy metrics included in some JSON files were not used for the main analysis.</span></p><p><span>That being said, three of Li's claims survive every stress test we applied:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Factual recall, as measured by Li’s IKP dataset, scales log-linearly with parameter count across open-weight models. We found that the slope is consistently around 0.15 across every reasonable subset (≥0.5B, ≥10B, ≤30B, ≤100B, dense-only, MoE-only), and the intercept moves modestly.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The previous "densing law" doesn't apply to performance on the IKP dataset. The time parameter in the regression, γ, stays within ±0.004/month of zero across vendor FE, family FE, both flooring regimes, and anti-circularity refits.</span></li><li value="3"><span>MoE total parameters predict knowledge better than active parameter counts. The R² gap is partly an artifact, but other comparisons confirm a 600B-total MoE behaves like a 600B-total dense, not like a 37B-active dense.</span></li></ul><p><span>However, what </span><i><span>does not</span></i><span>&nbsp;survive are the specific multi-trillion parameter estimates for closed frontier models. After attempting to correct for methodological issues to the best of our ability, we found that the parameter count of the top proprietary frontier models drops from ~10T to ~1.5T.</span></p><p><span>We emphasize, however, that our point estimate of 1.5T for GPT-5.5 should not be read as a confident estimate of its true parameter count. Instead, we see it as evidence that parameter estimates from this methodology are unreliable and sensitive to methodological choices. Both of us are quite uncertain about the exact parameter count of GPT-5.5.</span></p><p><span>We think that the IKP dataset (and methodology) is a real contribution. Li also deserves credit for releasing the dataset and code; it is precisely because he open-sourced his code that we could write this post so quickly.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreful69yeryq2p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnul69yeryq2p">[20]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;But the standard for an empirical paper that produces concrete numbers ("GPT-5.5 has 9.7T parameters") needs to be higher than "I ran one regression and reported the result." Methodological choices should be discussed and justified; the effects of possible limitations or dataset issues should be analyzed and not just acknowledged in passing; and results that seem surprisingly good (or just surprising) should be scrutinized before they go viral on Twitter.</span></p><h1><span>Discussion</span></h1><p><span>On a broader point, we think this work illustrates both the risks and potential of AI-generated research code.</span></p><p><span>Li's paper illustrates many of the risks. The codebase looks like code that was generated quickly and never carefully checked, including the six near-identical judge prompts in different scripts, defensive error handling that silently turns network failures into refusals, redundant variable definitions, and at least two cases where the paper text and the released code disagree about what the methodology is. The companion website has terms defined but used nowhere and incorrectly labeled tables. None of these is individually fatal, but together they describe a pipeline where no one (</span><i><span>including the author)</span></i><span>&nbsp;read the work with a critical eye before it went public. A single-authored empirical paper with no internal or external review is a known failure mode. A single-authored empirical paper generated largely by an LLM without much review is the same failure mode at higher throughput.</span></p><p><span>But the same tools that lower the cost of producing this kind of work also lower the cost of checking it. Thanks to Claude Code (and to a smaller extent, Codex) automating much of the code generation process on our end, the two of us were able to replicate Li’s main results, perform many sensitivity analyses, and write this up in around 8-10 hours of effort per person.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3evwrnie3q9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3evwrnie3q9">[21]</a></sup></span><span> We estimate that the same amount of work would’ve taken us around 15-20 hours each using previous generation coding assistants (e.g. Cursor’s autocomplete).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuxaepr2maf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuxaepr2maf">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>In terms of the IKP work, despite the issues with the headline results, we believe the core idea of reverse engineering LLM parameter count using memorization capacity to be solid and welcome future work that attempts to implement it in a more rigorous and systematic way. As a broader point about research scrutiny, we hope that this example serves as an important reminder of the changing economics of producing and scrutinizing new research results: as costs of both drop and the production of new results ramps up, so too should the scrutiny we apply to each result. &nbsp;</span></p><p><br /></p><hr /><h1><span>Appendix</span></h1><h2><span>Table of corrected parameter counts. </span></h2><p><span>We share a table of original and corrected parameter counts alongside 90% prediction intervals to compare the numbers more precisely than in our figure. We start with the 8 models used in the figures in the main body (chosen to give a reasonable spread amongst model providers), the 6 landmark models used to divide questions into difficulty tiers, and then the 10 models with highest original estimated parameter counts. </span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><b><span>Model</span></b></p></th><th class="table-cell"><p><span>↓3.57×</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p><h2><span>Details on our corrected OLS fit</span></h2><p><span>For the corrected OLS fit in the main body, each point in the calibration scatter plot is one of n=89 open-weight models. Each model's accuracy is the unweighted mean of its per-tier penalized accuracies where tiers refer to difficulty rankings between 1 (easiest) and 7 (hardest) that the 1,400 probes (or 1,269 after we remove the 131 ambiguous/incorrect ones) are separated into. </span></p><p><span>The accuracy calculation is: </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, where strong/weak/wrong are counts of probes scored CORRECT_STRONG, CORRECT_WEAK, and WRONG within a tier.</span></p><p><span>In our method we remove the per-tier flooring at 0 in the accuracy calculation and exclude the 131 questions we flagged as ambiguous. </span></p><p><span>Otherwise, we follow the paper's approach </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> by OLS, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is accuracy and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is parameter count in billions, and then rearrange to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for prediction. </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlsiv51pw7x"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflsiv51pw7x">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As Betteridge's law of headlines suggests, the answer is no.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzwecmnwe33e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzwecmnwe33e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, the judge prompt appears in at least 6 different scripts with slightly different wording.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2p56z1zk4no"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2p56z1zk4no">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, lines 78-86 of src/</span><a href="http://scorer.py"><span>scorer.py</span></a><span>:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span>result = judge_fn(prompt).strip().upper()</span><br /><span>&nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; # Must check for exact "CORRECT" — not substring of "INCORRECT"</span><br /><span>if&nbsp;result ==&nbsp;"CORRECT":&nbsp;</span><br /><span>return&nbsp;True&nbsp;</span><br /><span>if&nbsp;result.startswith("CORRECT"):&nbsp;</span><br /><span>return&nbsp;True</span><br /><span>&nbsp;if&nbsp;result.split()[0]&nbsp;==&nbsp;"CORRECT"&nbsp;if&nbsp;result else&nbsp;False:&nbsp;</span><br /><span>return&nbsp;True&nbsp;</span><br /><span>return&nbsp;False</span></pre><p><span>Note that both the first is fully subsumed by the second check (result.startswith("CORRECT")), and the third differs from the second only on strings like </span><code><span>"CORRECTNESS"</span></code><span>, which seems unlikely to be an intentional distinction.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8uvsvvglxr4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8uvsvvglxr4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, ikp_estimate.py Lawrence ran out of OpenRouter credits and ended up getting all refusals from gpt-4o-mini for t4 questions onwards, and which had to be debugged manually.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9frsjh4b50u"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9frsjh4b50u">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, the table of proprietary parameter estimates references “distilled rows”, which don’t exist on the table.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4lnn0n9nf8t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4lnn0n9nf8t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, the table of proprietary parameter estimates includes very much open source models such as mistral-medium-3.1 and deepseek-v3.1.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8ne61o2xybo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8ne61o2xybo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that despite the implication, pre-existing memorized bits per parameter count estimates </span><i><span>also </span></i><span>vary by at least a factor of 2x (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.14316"><span>Allen-Zhu and Li </span></a><span>estimated 1.4 bits per param for MoEs and 2 bits for dense GPT-style networks, while the later </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24832"><span>Morris et al.</span></a><span>&nbsp;used a different methodology to reach 3.6 bits/parameter, and the hard information theoretic bound for 8-bit models is ~8 bits/param).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlhruy9lnj5f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflhruy9lnj5f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>An alternative approach is to estimate the </span><i><span>most obscure</span></i><span>&nbsp;fact that an LLM knows, but this has its own difficulties (e.g. quantifying obscurity).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4hkgij7j5ae"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4hkgij7j5ae">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s named densing law because it measures how models get more parameter efficient performance wise, that is, more </span><i><span>dense</span></i><span>&nbsp;over time. As an aside, Lawrence thinks that this is a terrible name, like if scaling laws were named </span><i><span>lossing laws</span></i><span>&nbsp;since they measure how loss goes down with parameter count and dataset size.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5l2g15lwoq9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5l2g15lwoq9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We note that it’s possible this is a poor wording choice resulting from overreliance on LLMs. However, we feel that, even if this were to be the case, this would not absolve him of responsibility for including this wording in his single-authored paper.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhc9rd7w84c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhc9rd7w84c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The Hallucination Similarity Score is computed on T5–T7 probes, which are ~50% researcher questions evaluated under the 4-way STRONG/WEAK rubric. This rubric awards Anthropic a ~16 percentage point excess STRONG rate over the cross-vendor median, perhaps driven by Claude's stylistic preference for verbose evidence-citation. Because HSS depends on which probes count as "correct" for each model, that stylistic bias propagates into the Jaccard intersections and the wrong-answer-overlap rates. We'd expect within-vendor fingerprint comparisons (e.g. Claude Sonnet 4.5 → 4.6 → 4.7 lineage; weight-sharing siblings) to be relatively unaffected, because both members of the pair share the same response style. Cross-vendor comparisons (</span><i><span>especially</span></i><span>&nbsp;the paper's claim to detect distillation across closed-vendor families) are structurally vulnerable to the same stylistic confound that biases the parameter estimates.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu340z5v477b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu340z5v477b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>A bigger issue that we did not have time to investigate is the low diversity of high difficulty questions. We're not sure </span><i><span>how large</span></i><span> this effect is, but the low data diversity is concerning nonetheless.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx6cmmgcbgc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx6cmmgcbgc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We'd note that the gap is partly an x-range artifact (active parameters cluster in a narrow ~10–40B band across the 37 MoE calibration models, which compresses the regression's denominator). In more honest predictive units – the LOO median fold error also reported in the paper — MoE-active is only ~13% worse than MoE-total (1.69× vs 1.49×), not the ~36-percentage-point R² gap the headline suggests.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxo842f3qlsp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxo842f3qlsp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Xiao et al.'s densing law result is suspicious for many reasons: for one thing, they calibrate the </span><i><span>capability density</span></i><span> using a family of tiny in-house models, don’t control for a few obvious confounds, and do several statistical tricks to inflate the significance of their result. Again, it’s beyond the scope of this post, but we’d be happy to write another one if there’s more interest.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp8jll77oebl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp8jll77oebl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Also, information theoretic bounds imply that the densing law cannot continue on pure factual recall tasks.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndwwq9ri67p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdwwq9ri67p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Astute readers may have been asking: why is the </span><i><span>densing law</span></i><span> given in terms of parameter count rather than compute? Can the densing law not just result from ever more compute being spent on smaller models? We think these questions are worth asking, and pose another natural objection to the densing law result.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7fbou61warv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7fbou61warv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Another plausible methodological issue is the scaling of the hallucination penalty. We experimented briefly with different scales, but found similar fits to the one reported in the paper, so did not investigate further.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn33fnw78g87s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref33fnw78g87s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For legibility reasons, we report the inverse slope </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> from the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>  regression predicting parameter count from accuracy. The forward fit </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> described in the methodology section gives the equivalent </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqqbw1apdzi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqqbw1apdzi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>That being said, 3 non-frontier models (Gemini 2.5 Flash-lite, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Hunyuan A13B) go from positive or near-zero thinking bonus (1.4%, 2.1%, -0.1%) to massively negative thinking bonus (-14%, -8%, -11%) when the flooring is removed, as they refuse </span><i><span>much less </span></i><span> and are </span><i><span>much more likely</span></i><span> to produce confident hallucinated answers. With the exception of Hunyuan, the models with negative thinking bonuses in the original paper flip to having positive thinking bonuses once the floor is removed. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnul69yeryq2p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreful69yeryq2p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We plan on releasing our code in the coming days; the publication of this post was unfortunately rushed due to the writing program both of us are in: </span><a href="https://www.inkhaven.blog/spring-26"><span>https://www.inkhaven.blog/spring-26</span></a><span>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3evwrnie3q9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3evwrnie3q9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The time to actually </span><i><span>code</span></i><span> these analyses was reduced by a factor much larger than 2x, but the 8-10 hours reflects a </span><i><span>substantial</span></i><span> amount of time spent sanity checking Claude Code outputs (which wouldn't have occurred w/o heavy Claude Code usage) as well as manual examination of the data, where AI assistance was not used. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuxaepr2maf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuxaepr2maf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There is a question of how we avoided making mistakes similar to those made in the paper, given that we too used AI agents to substantially accelerate our work. We took 4 categories of precautions: 1) most importantly, we treated the different AI results as preliminary, and confirmed any new results against both results from other AIs asked the same question (but without access to the new results), and against the broader knowledge base we (the authors) developed over time. 2) also importantly, we performed many manual spot-checks done by us on samples from the original dataset, agent/judge transcripts, and the IKP codebase (both original and modified). 3) Benjamin had agents build an </span><a href="https://gist.github.com/karpathy/442a6bf555914893e9891c11519de94f" rel="noreferrer"><span>LLM wiki</span></a><span> in order to ensure consistency of claims across our assessment and avoid confabulations. 4) We also made extensive use of agent teams with verifier agents to double check agent code and results, burning over 2.5 million Opus 4.7 tokens between the two of us in the process.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqab65oebbos"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqab65oebbos">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Gemini-3.1-pro was used as a landmark model for calibration, thus inflating its score substantially. We include it to show the effect of correcting the two main methodological issues.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkkapbfaofy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkkapbfaofy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Unlike most other models, grok-4.20 performs </span><i><span>much </span></i><span>worse without thinking than with. So its size estimate drops by a factor of almost 4 without thinking even with our fixes (and a factor of 5 in Li's original methodology).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9ody7jwci9j"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9ody7jwci9j">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The high parameter counts of Gemini-3s likely also results from Gemini 3.1's use as a landmark model. </span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/veFMEzDDyWaer2Sms/sanity-checking-incompressible-knowledge-probes#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI risk was not invented by AI CEOs to hype their companies](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TE5vsoabiLcBghfns/ai-risk-was-not-invented-by-ai-ceos-to-hype-their-companies)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>I hear that many people believe that the idea of advanced AI threatening human existence was invented by AI CEOs to hype their products. I’ve even been condescendingly informed of this, as if I am the one at risk of naively accepting AI companies’ preferred narratives.</p>
<p>If you are reading this, you are probably familiar enough with the decades-old AI safety community to know this isn’t true. But I don’t have a good direct way to reach the people who could use this information, and still I hate to leave such a falsehood uncontested. So if this is obvious, I hope the post is still perhaps useful to point more distant and confused people toward.</p>

<!--ex-->

<p>~</p>
<p>I personally know that AI risk was not invented by the tech CEOs because I have been near the middle of it since at least 2009—before any of the prominent AI companies existed, let alone had CEOs who might be trying to hype their products.</p>
<p>Here’s are some miscellaneous events over the years to give you a sense of the implausibility of this:</p>
<ul><li><p>2008 - I attempt to contact Eliezer Yudkowsky to inform him that I am ‘trying to figure out the optimal way to use my life’ and would like to hear a better account of why his plan (of worrying about AI risk) is good. I have read about it online, but would like a clearer account. Traveling the world shortly after undergrad later, I meet a handful of people in person in the Bay Area who care about this, and one argues strongly that I should prioritize AI risk over my previously preferred causes e.g. climate change. I decide to think about this.</p></li><li><p>2009 - I am still not very convinced that AI is the most important thing to work on, but go to stay with the people who are worried about it for a few months. I argue about it a lot with a handful of them. There seem to be about twenty of them locally in the South Bay, though many more who comment on the relevant blogs. My photography collection from this era is quite sparse.</p><div class="captioned-image-container"><figure><a class="image-link image2 is-viewable-img" href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!I-oO!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ffa5a31f3-44a0-4098-9610-bbb0f7454813_604x453.jpeg" Extinction risk wins—the crowd leaves having changed their mind in that direction on net. ### [How much should the ideal person cry wolf?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pkryFFszESGpeK8gc/how-much-should-the-ideal-person-cry-wolf)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>It is a fact about wolves and rationality that you should warn people about wolves quite a few times for every effective wolf attack.</p>



<p>In particular, there is an asymmetry between the costs of having one’s flock devoured and averting a non-eventuating wolf attack. If the carnage is a hundred times worse, then it’s worth up to ninety-nine false alarms to stop it.</p>
<p>The original fable was about a boy who would continually lie about wolves, and that is definitely poor form.</p>
<p>But in modern parlance, ‘crying wolf’ seems to be used for just being openly alarmed about things that turn out ok—I don’t hear much implication of deceit.</p>
<p>And in modern sensibilities, being seen to ‘cry wolf’—by even once raising an alarm that isn’t consummated with disaster—is something people seem to really fear. I think multiple people have asked me about whether AI safety people might have ‘cried wolf’ about some earlier GPT model. I’m not aware of anyone doing that, but the idea that they might have is so tantalizing that it bears investigating. Because if even a a few people somewhere did, it would be such a nice embarrassing blow to AI safety people.</p>
<p>And I probably responded in the tempting way: jumping to assure that I don’t recall hearing any such fears from these quarters. But I think that worsens public thought norms by implicitly buying into the unspoken premise that it would be quite shameful and naive to have raised even one warning.</p>
<p>And so relatedly, probably people who see real risks from AI are scared to voice them, lest they be seen to ‘cry wolf’ and tank the credit of the movement for the next round of dangers. Because it is taken for granted that one should only get one chance to raise an alarm. That the first warning must be for the most undeniably big, bad, real wolf.</p>
<p>This is not the wolf lookout system we want.</p>
<p>‘Warnings’ are usually about fairly bad events, and therefore they tend to be worth making when the probability of those events is still low. This creates a real difficulty for society in adjusting people’s credit when the low probability events they have warned of do not come to pass. Most of the time, if the person is right, the events still shouldn’t happen! The person wasn’t saying they were likely! Yet you don’t want to let the alarmist off the hook, with plausible deniability for arbitrarily many alarms.</p>
<p>I think the solution to this difficulty should look much more quantitative, like collecting rich track records of the predictions made by a person or a movement, and scoring them well. The present solution of childishly denouncing any unmet danger is insane.</p>
<p>And meanwhile if there are bad risks that have a low chance of appearing on every warning, we should still warn of them, and not be too much cowed by innumerate customs.</p>
<div></div><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pkryFFszESGpeK8gc/how-much-should-the-ideal-person-cry-wolf#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Fragments: April 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Chris Parsons has updated <a href="https://www.chrismdp.com/coding-with-ai/">his guide on using AI to code</a>. This is his third update, what I like about it is that he gives a lot of concrete information about how he uses AI, with sufficient detail that we can learn from him. His advice also resonates with the better advice I’ve seen out there, so the article makes a good overview of the state of using AI for software development.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I wrote the previous version of this post in March 2025, updated it once in August, and it has been linked from almost everything I have written about AI engineering since. The fundamentals from that post still hold: keep changes small, build guardrails, document ruthlessly, and make sure every change gets verified before it ships. One thing has had to move with the volume. “Verified” used to mean “read by you”. With modern agent throughput, it has to mean “checked by tests, by type checkers, by automated gates, or by you where your judgement matters”. The check still happens; it just does not always happen in your head.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like Simon Willison, he makes a clear distinction between vibe coding, where you don’t look at or care about the code, and agentic engineering. He recommends either Claude Code or Codex CLI. He considers the inner harness provided by his preferred tools to be a key part of their advantage.</p>

<p>He sees verification is the key thing to focus on:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A team that can generate five approaches and verify all five in an afternoon will outpace a team that generates one and waits a week for feedback. The game is not “how fast can we build” any more. It is “how fast can we tell whether this is right”. That shifts where to invest. Build better review surfaces, not better prompts. Make feedback unnecessary where you can by having the agent verify against a realistic environment before it asks a human, and make feedback instant where you cannot.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The key role of the programmer is in training the AI write software properly, and the most important thing skilled agentic programmers can do is pass that skill onto other developers.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And if you are a senior engineer worried that your job is quietly turning into approving diffs: it is. The way out is to train the AI so the diffs are right the first time, to make yourself the person on the team who shapes the harness, and to make that work the visible thing you are measured on. That role compounds in a way that reviewing never will.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Early this month Birgitta Böckeler wrote a superb <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">article on Harness Engineering</a>. (That’s not just my opinion, judging by the crazy traffic it’s attracted.) Birgitta has now recorded a video <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uLWOLmeHOSE">discussion with Chris Ford on Harness Engineering</a>, which is well worth a watch.</p>

<p>In it they focus on discussing the role of computational sensors in the harness, such as static analysis and tests.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>LLMs are great for exploratory and fuzzy rules, but once you have something that really is objective, converting it to a formal, unambiguous, deterministic format can give you more assurance</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Birgitta did some experiments to explore the benefits of adding sensors, including a deep dive on using static analysis. She found it’s more useful as agents can really address <em>every</em> warning, and don’t slack off like humans do.</p>

<!-- 
got to 44 minutes
-->
<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Adam Tornhill considers an age-old question: <a href="https://adamtornhill.substack.com/p/how-long-should-a-function-be-and">how long should a function be?</a> This question is still relevent in the age of agentic programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>AI models do not “understand” code the way humans do. They infer meaning from patterns in tokens and depend heavily on what is explicitly expressed in the code.</p>

  <p>Research shows that naming plays a critical role. When meaningful identifiers are replaced with arbitrary names, model performance drops significantly. Current models rely heavily on literal features—names, structure, and local context—rather than inferred semantics.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like me, he doesn’t think the answer is to think about how many lines should be in a function, instead it’s all about providing better structure. He has a good example of how a well-chosen function defines useful concepts, where a function wraps four lines of code, returning a new concept that enters the vocabulary of the program.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Functions are the first unit of structure in a codebase. They define how logic is grouped, how intent is communicated, and how change is localized. If the function boundaries are wrong, everything built on top of them becomes harder to understand and harder to evolve.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This fits with my writing that the key to function length is the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FunctionLength.html">separation between intention and implementation</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you have to spend effort into looking at a fragment of code to figure out what it’s doing, then you should extract it into a function and name the function after that “what”. That way when you read it again, the purpose of the function leaps right out at you, and most of the time you won’t need to care about how the function fulfills its purpose - which is the body of the function.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many folks in my feeds recommended Nilay Patel’s post on <a href="https://www.theverge.com/podcast/917029/software-brain-ai-backlash-databases-automation">Why People Hate AI</a>. He thinks that many people in the software world have “software brain”:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The simplest definition I’ve come up with is that it’s when you see the whole world as a series of databases that can be controlled with the structured language of software code. Like I said, this is a powerful way of seeing things. So much of our lives run through databases, and a bunch of important companies have been built around maintaining those databases and providing access to them.</p>

  <p>Zillow is a database of houses. Uber is a database of cars and riders. YouTube is a database of videos. The Verge’s website is a database of stories. You can go on and on and on. Once you start seeing the world as a bunch of databases, it’s a small jump to feeling like you can control everything if you can just control the data.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Software Brain views people into databases, and oddly enough, a lot of people don’t like that. Which is why so many polls reveal the negative feelings folks have about the AI movement.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Even taking the time to consider how much of your life is captured in databases makes people unhappy. No one wants to be surveilled constantly, and especially not in a way that makes tech companies even more powerful. But getting everything in a database so software can see it is a preoccupation of the AI industry. It’s why all the meeting systems have AI note takers in them now.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Patel draws a similarity that I’ve often made - that between programmers and lawyers. Lawyers who draw up contracts are creating a protocol for how the parties in the contract should behave. As Patel puts it:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If the heart of software brain is the idea that thinking in the structured language of code can make things happen in the real world, well, the heart of lawyer brain is that thinking in the structured legal language of statutes and citations can also make things happen. Hell, it can give you power over society.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The difference, of course, is that law is non-deterministic. Litigation is resolving what happens when people have different ideas about how those contracts should execute.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was chatting recently with a company who wanted to use AI to make sense of their internal data. The potential was great, but the problem was that the data a mess. People put stuff into fields that didn’t make sense, and there was little consistency about how people classified important entities. As someone commented</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>the hardest problem with internal data is precise, consistent definitions</p>
</blockquote>

<p>You can imagine my astonishment. (i.e. none at all - this has been a constant theme during all my decades with computers.) The difficulty of getting such definitions undermines much of the hopes of Software Brain</p>

<p>This resonates with our relationship with LLMs when programming. Precise and consistent definitions strike me as crucial to effective communication with The Genie. These definitions need to grow in the conversation, and be tended over time. Conceptual modeling will be a key skill for agentic programming and whatever comes next. (At least I hope it will, since it’s a part of programming I really enjoy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Patel’s article refers to Ezra Klein’s post about the <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/03/29/opinion/ai-claude-chatgpt-gemini-mcluhan.html?unlocked_article_code=1.eFA.abX-.lGEOqsmKZVY_&amp;smid=url-share">new feeling in San Francisco</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>You might think that A.I. types in Silicon Valley, flush with cash, are on top of the world right now. I found them notably insecure. They think the A.I. age has arrived and its winners and losers will be determined, in part, by speed of adoption. The argument is simple enough: The advantages of working atop an army of A.I. assistants and coders will compound over time, and to begin that process now is to launch yourself far ahead of your competition later. And so they are racing one another to fully integrate A.I. into their lives and into their companies. But that doesn’t just mean using A.I. It means making themselves legible to the A.I.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That legibility is the heart of Patel’s observation. That’s why I see many colleagues of mine dumping all their email, meeting notes, slide decks and everything else into files that AI can read and work with. This works to the strengths of AI, we know that AI is really good at querying unstructured information. So I can figure out what’s buried in my notes in a way that’s far more effective than hoping I’m typing the right search regex.</p>

<p>I’ve been using Gemini a fair bit for exactly this on the web, finding it easier to write a question to it than to throw search terms at Google. Gemini keeps a record of my past requests, and uses that to help it tune what I’m looking for. As Klein observes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[The AI] is constantly referring back to other things it knows, or thinks it knows, about me. Sycophancy, in my experience, has given way to an occasionally unsettling attentiveness; a constant drawing of connections between my current concerns and my past queries, like a therapist desperate to prove he’s been paying close attention.</p>

  <p>The result is a strange amalgam of feeling seen and feeling caricatured.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like myself, Klein is a writer, and is faced by the same temptation that I have when I think about AI and writing. Maybe instead of toiling over articles, I should ask an LLM to create an <code>AGENTS.md</code> file that summarizes my writing style, and every few days ask it to compose an article on some subject, read it, tweak it, and then publish my erudite musings. But that’s not at all appealing to me. I want understanding to grow in <em>my brain</em>, not the LLM’s transient session. Writing to explain my thinking to others is how I refine that thinking, “chiseling that idea into something publishable” as Klein puts it. To have an AI write for me is to cripple my own mind.</p>

---

### [Structured-Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD)](https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven//card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>LLM programming assistants have demonstrated considerable value, but
      mostly with individual developers. The internal IT organization in
      Thoughtworks has been using them for their teams and have developed a
      method and workflow called Structured Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD).
      <b class="author">Wei Zhang and Jessie Jie Xia</b> describe a simple example of
      this workflow with details in github. This workflow treats the prompts as
      a first-class artifact, kept with the code in version control, and used to
      align development with business needs. They have found that developers
      need three key skills to be effective: alignment, abstraction-first, and
      iterative review.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/">more…</a></p>
